#5213. Binance Hackers Swap About $50 Million of Stolen XRP Mostly Into Bitcoin, Elliptic Says

Hackers tied to the record Binance theft have begun converting a large slice of the stolen XRP into bitcoin, blockchain analytics firm Elliptic said in an update dated through Friday, with about 42 million XRP — roughly $50 million — already swapped, mostly through THORChain.

Converted bitcoin from other stolen assets nearly doubled overnight to about 1,650 BTC, worth some $124 million, Elliptic wrote. Most of that jump came from the XRP route. About 192 million XRP, roughly $226 million, remained unswapped in addresses linked to the Binance theft.

The XRP was part of the Oct. 29 drain that Binance later put at $6.34 billion. Between 20:05 and 20:09 UTC that day, five hot wallets sent 233.46 million XRP to an attacker address. The FBI has attributed the campaign to North Korea’s TraderTraitor group. Binance has reopened withdrawals in stages and said it has frozen or intercepted about $2.85 billion while covering customer losses from its own funds.

The stolen XRP sat quiet at first. Early on Nov. 2, between 02:12 and 04:00 UTC, all about 233 million XRP moved into six new addresses, where it stayed until Nov. 5, Elliptic said. From the afternoon of Nov. 5, two of those six addresses began sending XRP on to dozens of new accounts that swapped it, mostly through THORChain into bitcoin.

A small amount of ether also went through THORChain into bitcoin that evening. The main bitcoin and ether holdings from the theft — the largest pools — have still not moved, Elliptic said. That pattern matches what the firm saw after the Bybit hack: turn freezable and mid-liquidity tokens into bitcoin first, then attempt cash-out later through OTC brokers.

The broader laundering trail is already vast. Since the Binance theft, Elliptic has counted more than 20,000 wallets on 16 blockchains, at least 36 services and at least 65,000 transactions — roughly one new wallet every 30 seconds and one transaction every 10 seconds. About $3.11 billion remains in addresses linked to the Binance theft, mostly as ether and bitcoin. A negligible amount has likely been cashed out so far.

Issuers can freeze many stablecoins. They cannot freeze XRP the same way, and they cannot freeze bitcoin. After Tether and Circle locked down large stablecoin tranches on Oct. 29, the attacker prioritized assets that sit outside that kill switch. Swapping XRP into bitcoin continues that logic: bitcoin is harder to claw back once it is fragmented across fresh addresses.

All of the stolen SOL, about $87 million at Elliptic’s prices, has already been swapped, bridged or mixed, with much of it ending as bitcoin. Most early Tron laundering — about $220 million in TRX — has also run, a flow Bitcoin Magazine noted when covering the quiet shutdown of the T3 Financial Crime Unit before the hack. Only small parts of the main ether and NEAR piles have moved.

U.S. bank supervisors have already checked in with lenders after the Binance breach. For bitcoin markets, the nearer question is how much more of the remaining ~192 million XRP — and eventually the parked ether — gets turned into BTC before anyone tries to cash out.

The test now is whether THORChain operators and other bridges keep blocking the published attacker addresses fast enough to slow the XRP-to-bitcoin pipe, or whether the rest of that pile follows the same overnight path the first $50 million already took.

#5212. US Voters Back AI Data Center Restrictions in At Least 29 Communities

US Voters Back AI Data Center Restrictions in At Least 29 Communities

Voters in at least 29 U.S. jurisdictions backed bans or restrictions on data-center development Tuesday, according to The Washington Post, dealing a new setback to the massive infrastructure buildout behind artificial intelligence.

The measures ranged from outright prohibitions to requirements for additional public approval. Some were nonbinding advisory votes, meaning their passage does not automatically stop construction.

Ballotpedia tracked 41 data-center measures on November 3 ballots across 10 states. Twenty-eight were framed so that a yes vote would curb development, while 13 would permit or support projects, meaning rejecting one of the latter could also count as a defeat for developers.

Ohio produced some of the clearest results. All 10 charter measures backed by the grassroots group Conserve Ohio passed, according to the Post, and more than three-quarters of voters in Hubbard, Ohio, supported a ban on data centers.

The negative sentiment is so big that voters in Granville, Trenton, and Sunbury backed restrictions with margins of roughly 78% to 82%.

In Illinois, nearly 80% of voters in Joliet Township rejected an advisory proposal supporting AI data centers. In Janesville, Wisconsin, more than 60% approved a requirement for another public vote before major development at a former General Motors site linked to a proposed data center.

These facilities house the chips and servers used to train and run AI systems. Their appetite for electricity has turned proposed campuses into local fights over utility bills and grid capacity, while opponents also raise concerns about water use, noise and deals struck without public scrutiny.

AI companies are aware of the growing backlash and are already working to counter it. Some are stepping up lobbying efforts, while others are turning to public relations campaigns to reshape public perception. OpenAI, for instance, has sought to rebrand data centers as “compute campuses,” in an effort to distance the facilities from the negative connotations increasingly associated with them.

The votes arrived alongside victories by politicians promising tougher oversight of data centers. Democrat Gina Hinojosa defeated Republican Gov. Greg Abbott in Texas after campaigning for a moratorium on new projects, while Ohio's governor-elect Amy Acton also supports a pause and stricter rules.

Industry leaders have warned that blanket restrictions would put U.S. AI investment at risk. BlackRock CEO Larry Fink said last month that a broad moratorium could have serious economic consequences. "If we have a moratorium of some sort in this country, we will be in a recession," he said.

#5210. Rule to Allow Crypto in 401(k)s Arrives at White House for Final Review

A final DOL rule that would ease alternative assets such as private equity, real estate and cryptocurrency into 401(k) retirement plans arrived at the White House on Thursday for review, Bloomberg Law reported, a procedural step that usually precedes public release.

The Office of Information and Regulatory Affairs logged the package on Nov. 5 under the title “Fiduciary Duties In Selecting Designated Investment Alternatives,” RIN 1210-AC38. The filing lists the DOL’s Employee Benefits Security Administration as the authoring office, marks the stage as a final rule, flags it as economically significant and notes that there is no legal deadline for completion.

The DOL unveiled the proposal on March 30. In that draft, plan fiduciaries who follow a documented process when adding private assets to a 401(k) menu would get a legal safe harbor under the Employee Retirement Income Security Act of 1974.

The six factors named in the proposal are performance, fees, liquidity, valuation, performance benchmarks and complexity. Satisfying them does not force plans to offer crypto. It lowers the litigation risk for employers that decide they want to.

JUST IN: 🇺🇸 The White House is now reviewing a new rule which will allow Americans to invest in crypto through their 401(k) retirement plans 👀 pic.twitter.com/uXGGIXEzdC

President Donald Trump’s Aug. 7, 2025, executive order, “Democratizing Access to Alternative Assets for 401(k) Investors,” directed the Labor secretary to reexamine ERISA guidance and, where appropriate, propose rules with “appropriately calibrated safe harbors.” The order defined alternative assets to include private-market equity and debt, real estate, holdings in actively managed vehicles that invest in digital assets, commodities, infrastructure and lifetime-income strategies.

The March proposal implements that order. A DOL fact sheet said prudence under ERISA is process-based and that fiduciaries retain discretion to select any designated investment alternative if they follow the outlined review. Deputy Labor Secretary Keith Sonderling cast the approach as neutral: “The department’s days of picking winners and losers are over.”

That stance reverses a prior chill. In May 2025 the DOL rescinded its March 2022 compliance release that told fiduciaries to use “extreme care” before putting cryptocurrency on a 401(k) menu. The department said that phrase was not found in ERISA and restored a context-specific standard.

Later, it was reported that Sens. Bernie Sanders and Elizabeth Warren pressed the DOL to abandon the crypto-friendly path. House Republicans have separately pushed to codify Trump’s crypto order in statute.

OIRA review under Executive Order 12866 is the last interagency checkpoint before a final rule can be published in the Federal Register. Bloomberg Law said the arrival signals the package is nearing completion. The same office cleared the proposed version in late March before the DOL published it; that earlier pass took roughly two months after the January submission, per prior Bloomberg coverage of the roughly $12 trillion 401(k) market.

What is not yet public is the final text itself. Whether the six-factor safe harbor survived comment intact, how digital-asset vehicles are described, and what effective date the DOL chose will only be clear when OIRA returns the rule and the department posts it. Comment volume on the proposal was heavy; industry and labor groups have argued opposite sides of the prudence standard for months.

For bitcoin, the stakes sit in plan menus rather than spot ETF flows. Trump’s August order helped frame digital assets as a retirement-access issue for more than 90 million defined-contribution savers. A finalized DOL safe harbor would not put bitcoin and crypto on every lineup overnight. It would give plan committees a clearer map if they want exposure through an asset-allocation sleeve that holds digital assets.

The test now is how fast OIRA clears the economically significant package — and whether the published final rule still gives fiduciaries the process cover the March draft promised, or whether the comment fight rewrote the safe harbor before the White House stamps it.

#5208. US Bank Regulator Issues First AI Framework as Banks Battle Rogue Agents

US Bank Regulator Issues First AI Framework as Banks Battle Rogue Agents

The U.S. Office of the Comptroller of the Currency issued its first supervisory framework for artificial intelligence in banking today, telling examiners to focus less on how powerful an AI system is and more on what happens when it makes a mistake.

The OCC's framework addresses that very concern, although the regulator has not linked its issuance to those specific incidents.

In a section covering operational resilience, the agency warns that external agents operated by customers, fintechs, or other businesses could create risks for banks even when the banks don't control those systems.

It suggests examiners consider measures including identity verification, delegated permissions, transaction limits, monitoring, and the ability to suspend or revoke access.

The guidelines aim to tackle the dilemma of how to let customers use AI assistants to manage their finances without giving autonomous software unrestricted access to accounts that banks are already facing.

The new OCC guidance arrives less than two weeks after reports that major banks were discovering unauthorized third-party AI agents operating inside customer accounts, sometimes taking actions that appeared to go beyond users' instructions.

Morgan Stanley was blocking outside agents, while JPMorgan, Bank of America, and Citi were restricting access, according to an Axios report on October 22. Bank executives described the situation as "horrifying," raising questions about who controls an autonomous agent once it has access to someone's money.

The OCC's framework makes a distinction between an AI system's technical sophistication and the damage it could cause.

A simple agent authorized to initiate financial transactions may present greater risk than a more advanced model limited to writing internal documents, the regulator said.

Under the 22-page framework, examiners can consider the consequences of failure, whether an action can be reversed, and how much control humans retain over the system.

The agency outlines four illustrative risk tiers. Fully autonomous payment agents and real-time credit decision systems fall into the highest category, while basic internal productivity tools occupy the lowest.

Higher-risk systems may warrant tighter restrictions on autonomy, independent testing, and human approval before consequential actions can proceed.

The framework also addresses AI systems that continue operating while making harmful decisions. Suggested safeguards include circuit breakers, rate limits, restricted permissions, and emergency shutdown controls.

Those considerations are especially relevant to the outside agents described in Axios's earlier reporting, where banks struggled to distinguish legitimate customer-authorized software from systems operating beyond their intended permissions.

The OCC stressed that the document does not create new regulatory requirements or enforceable standards for banks.

Instead, it gives examiners a way to evaluate AI within existing operational, compliance, and cybersecurity risk categories. Banks are not required to establish a separate AI risk category.

The document uses the mandatory term "Super Intelligence," which the Donald Trump administration insists on using in place of “artificial intelligence,” but its scope includes conventional AI and “autonomous agents” rather than only hypothetical systems with intelligence beyond human capabilities.

The framework covers AI used in payments, fraud detection, customer service, and other banking operations. It also extends its risk considerations to external AI agents connecting through bank channels.

The OCC said it intends to update the guidance as the technology develops. For now, its illustrative risk scale puts autonomous payment agents in the highest-risk category, regardless of how well they perform in testing.

#5206. Anchorage Digital seeks CFTC swap dealer registration for crypto options business

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The firm plans to offer European-style bitcoin and ether options with maturities of up to one year if its application is approved.

Institutional crypto platform Anchorage Digital has filed an application to register a swap dealer with the Commodity Futures Trading Commission as it looks to expand its institutional crypto options business.

The application was submitted to the National Futures Association through Anchorage Digital Swap Dealer, LLC, according to a statement shared exclusively with The Block.

Anchorage is now awaiting registration while the NFA reviews its application. CEO and co-founder Nathan McCauley offered no estimate of when that process might finish, but said the company would share further updates as appropriate. "We aren't in a position to speculate on timing," he told The Block.

If the application is approved, McCauley said Anchorage Digital Swap Dealer expects to offer European-style options on bitcoin Bitcoin (BTC) and ether Ethereum (ETH), with maturities ranging up to one year. The firm intends to add more products later, he added, subject to its own approval process and regulatory requirements.

McCauley said that once registration is complete, institutional clients would be able to trade digital asset derivatives with a CFTC-registered counterparty alongside use of Anchorage Digital's existing custody and other services.

Much of Anchorage's existing options business growth has come from public companies holding bitcoin and ether on their balance sheets, the firm said in the statement, while asset managers and funds using options to manage their exposure have also contributed. McCauley would not disclose specific trading volumes or growth figures when asked by The Block, and declined to explain how Anchorage had previously arranged options trades for clients.

"What we can say is that institutional demand for regulated access to digital asset derivatives continues to grow, and establishing a dedicated swap dealer entity is part of how we're building the infrastructure to meet it," he said.

Anchorage Digital Bank, N.A. became the first federally chartered digital asset bank in 2021 and is regulated by the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency. The swap dealer entity would face CFTC requirements for capital, risk management, and margin, using the same framework the Dodd-Frank Act applied to the banks for interest rate, credit, and FX markets, the firm said.

"It will bring our options business under the same federal framework as the dealers they already face," McCauley said in the statement.

In March, Anchorage Digital added collateral management to its Atlas platform, including 24/7 monitoring and automated margin calls for loans, derivatives, and other institutional transactions, The Block reported at the time. It also acquired Securitize's wealth management unit in December to expand its federally regulated crypto services for registered investment advisors.

#5205. Strive (ASST) Posts $474.8 Million Common Net Income as Bitcoin Yield Hits 63.2%

Strive reported $474.8 million of net income attributable to common stockholders for the third quarter ended Sept. 30, a print that arrived Friday alongside a company claim that Class A total return had outperformed bitcoin by 103.7% year to date through the same date.

GAAP net income was $509.0 million, or $5.24 a share on a fully diluted basis. Almost all of that came from a $533.7 million unrealized gain on digital assets marked to fair value. Operating revenue for the quarter was just $2.7 million, so the earnings line is a treasury mark, not a fee business.

In a post on X that led with the outperformance figure, Strive also highlighted a year-to-date bitcoin yield of 63.2% and a bitcoin dollar gain of $9.00 per assumed diluted share outstanding, both measured through Sept. 30. Chairman and CEO Matthew Cole tied those metrics to the stock’s relative showing against the asset.

“Strive has continued to successfully execute on its treasury management strategy, delivering a strong bitcoin yield year to date in 2026, which I believe is the primary driver of our common equity’s outperformance relative to Bitcoin so far this year,” Cole said in the earnings release.

Bitcoin yield, as Strive defines it, is the percentage change in bitcoin per share over a period — not a cash yield. On that measure the company posted 18.5% in the third quarter alone and 19.3% from Oct. 1 through Oct. 30, after the SATS preferred debut.

Strive acquired 8,136 bitcoin in the third quarter at an average cost of about $78,885 and 20,373 bitcoin over the nine months ended Sept. 30. Another 15,124 coins followed between Oct. 1 and Oct. 30. Holdings stood at 28,000 bitcoin on Sept. 30 and 43,124 by Oct. 30, keeping Strive among the largest public corporate stacks after recent multi-thousand-coin buys and smaller weekly adds that still trailed Strategy.

Digital assets at fair value were $2.34 billion on the Sept. 30 balance sheet. Cash had risen to $490.4 million by Oct. 30, and Strive still held Strategy’s variable-rate STRC preferred with a fair value of about $50.1 million.

The capital stack that funded the buys is mostly perpetual preferreds. On Oct. 20 Strive closed a registered offering of 7 million shares of its 15% Series A perpetual preferred (SATS) for about $609 million in net proceeds. Preferred dividends of $34.2 million in the quarter are what turned the $509.0 million GAAP profit into the $474.8 million common figure.

Strip the story to nine-month GAAP and the picture flips. For the nine months ended Sept. 30, Strive posted a net loss attributable to common stockholders of $88.4 million. The third-quarter profit is real under fair-value accounting, but it is concentrated in one mark-to-market line.

ASST itself has not moved in a straight line with the company’s bitcoin-yield scoreboard. Shares closed at $20.59 on Thursday, down about 10% on the day, and traded near $20.70 early Friday. That leaves the stock roughly 29% below its level a year earlier, when Yahoo Finance data put the close near $29, and about 33% under its Oct. 21 local high of $30.75.

The 52-week range on Yahoo Finance runs from $7.02 to $34.40. Year to date, the price path from the Jan. 2 close near $17.30 into the mid-$20s is a gain of roughly 20%, with a February low near the 52-week floor and a September push back toward $30. Through Sept. 30 — the cutoff on Strive’s outperformance claim — ASST had risen about 70% from that January close while bitcoin was down roughly 7% over the same window on Yahoo Finance data, a wide gap even before the company’s own total-return methodology.

The longer chart still carries the scar of 2025, when Asset Entities became the public shell for Strive’s bitcoin-treasury reverse merger and the share price spiked into triple digits on weekly closes before collapsing. Investors who held through that conversion saw a different ride than the tidy year-to-date relative-return figure Strive put at the top of Friday’s note.

Cole said the recent SATS issuance leaves Strive “never been in a better position to generate long-term returns in excess of Bitcoin for our common equity shareholders.” The test for ASST from here is whether another quarter of bitcoin yield and preferred-funded buys can reopen the gap to bitcoin after a year in which the stock still sits well below where it traded twelve months ago, even as the treasury has climbed past 30,000 then 40,000 bitcoin.

#5204. Coinbase Says 99% of Customer Crypto Is Offline and Out of Hackers’ Reach

Coinbase Says 99% of Customer Crypto Is Offline and Out of Hackers’ Reach

Coinbase is moving to reassure customers that what happened at Binance can’t easily happen there.

In a blog post Wednesday, the U.S. exchange said it wasn’t affected by last week’s hack of Binance, in which attackers drained about $6.4 billion from the rival exchange’s hot wallets.

Coinbase said it keeps more than 99% of customer assets in cold storage, meaning offline wallets that can’t be reached over the internet. It moves funds into internet-connected hot wallets only as customers need them.

The Binance incident “has understandably left customers asking” how Coinbase guards against a similar attack, the post said.

Coinbase laid out several layers of protection. Private keys for its cold wallets are split into pieces and never fully reassembled, even when signing transactions, and no single employee has standing access to the hot-wallet signing service. Strict limits cap how fast funds can move from cold to hot wallets, and unusual withdrawal spikes are held until staff review them. The exchange can also trigger “kill switches” that take signing keys offline entirely.

The company said it assumes employees will be targeted by phishing, so its controls are designed so that a stolen password, session or device can’t be used on its own, and sensitive actions require multiple approvals.

That focus on insiders carries weight for Coinbase. In May 2025, it disclosed that criminals had bribed overseas customer support agents to steal personal data on less than 1% of its monthly transacting users, then demanded a $20 million ransom that Coinbase refused to pay.

Last week, Coinbase said a security incident at analytics vendor Amplitude exposed contact data for a “small subset” of customers, though funds weren’t affected.

Coinbase also said customer assets are held 1:1 and aren’t lent out unless customers direct it to, and it reported $8.6 billion in cash and cash equivalents and $377 billion in assets on its platform as of Sept. 30. It flagged artificial intelligence as a fast-changing threat and pledged further security investment.

The Binance theft, which the FBI has attributed to North Korea’s TraderTraitor group, has rattled the industry. Blockchain analytics firm Elliptic found that the laundering spanned about 12,000 wallets in under four days, while stablecoin issuers froze about 44% of the haul.

Separately, Brazil’s Mercado Bitcoin halted withdrawals after a $40 million hack, and French bank Crédit Mutuel Arkéa recently confirmed a cyberattack that led to a $133 million crypto theft.

#5202. Canary’s Injective ETF debuts on Nasdaq with exposure to staking rewards

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

An Injective affiliate recently became an SEC-registered transfer agent amid an expanding tokenization push.

Canary Capital has launched the Canary Staked INJ ETF, offering investors exposure to Injective's native INJ token. The fund will trade on the Nasdaq under the ticker INJC.

The ETF seeks to earn additional INJ through in Injective's proof-of-stake validation process, with net staking rewards reflected in its net asset value.

"Injective was designed from the ground up for trading, tokenization, markets, and financial applications," said Canary Capital CEO Steven McClurg in a press release. "We believe investors are increasingly looking past the largest digital assets and toward the specialized networks building the next generation of on-chain financial infrastructure."

Injective is a Layer 1 blockchain focused on onchain financial applications, including trading, derivatives, and tokenization. Its native token was up more than 5% on Thursday.

In August, Injective's affiliate, Injective Institutional Services, became an SEC-registered transfer agent amid an expanding tokenization push.

"In terms of U.S. licensing, I think transfer agent is basically the very start," Injective CEO Eric Chen told The Block last month, noting that "an Injective institution can actually be the clearinghouse for settlement."

Injective says it has tokenized over $1 billion in assets to date. As of Oct. 22, Injective had processed more than 3.13 billion transactions and permanently burned more than 7.27 million INJ across its monthly token buybacks.

"Investors can now gain exposure to INJ through the brokerage accounts they already use," Chen said Thursday in a release. "We built Injective to bring financial markets onchain. Canary's launch connects that mission to a broader base of investors, giving them a familiar way to access the asset that powers Injective."

There are two other proposed ETF applications tied to INJ: the 21Shares Injective ETF and the REX-Osprey INJ Staking ETF.

#5200. Former FBI Agent Pleads Guilty to Stealing $2.47 Million in Crypto From Investigations

A former FBI agent pleaded guilty Thursday to stealing about $2.47 million in cryptocurrency with wallet credentials the bureau had collected in its own cases, the U.S. Attorney’s Office for the Eastern District of Virginia announced.

Patrick Steven Yaroch, 37, of Ashburn, Virginia, entered pleas to wire fraud and money laundering in federal court in Alexandria. U.S. District Judge Patricia Tolliver Giles accepted the plea. He faces up to 20 years in prison and is scheduled for sentencing on March 4, 2027.

Court papers put the illicit haul at $2,474,380.66. Prosecutors say Yaroch made roughly 100 fraudulent transfers using credentials tied to 35 separate FBI investigations, drawing on wallets controlled by investigation subjects, adversarial nation-state actors and at least one wallet the FBI itself had created for an operation.

The Justice Department Office of the Inspector General had flagged the case in August after Yaroch’s arrest on a criminal complaint. That earlier account described a smaller set of transfers — about 10 moves totaling roughly $1 million — and noted that Yaroch self-reported the activity to two department employees on July 29, 2026. The plea figures are larger.

From 2017 through early 2025, Yaroch worked in the FBI Boston Division on a squad focused on an adversarial nation-state. From February 2025 through July 31, 2026, he served in the FBI’s Counterintelligence and Espionage Division at headquarters, with access to systems that stored cryptocurrency credentials gathered in investigations.

Beginning no later than December 2024, Yaroch used that access to move coins out of those wallets and into ones he controlled. After the transfers, he swapped the stolen cryptocurrency into other digital assets and ran further transactions meant to obscure its source, nature and ownership.

Between Nov. 25, 2025, and Jan. 13, 2026, he moved about $93,082 into one of his accounts, then shuffled funds among other accounts to cover debts, including credit cards, car payments and loans, the office said.

FOX 5 DC, citing the same U.S. Attorney’s Office release, noted the Portugal planning that appears in the plea papers. Yaroch asked ChatGPT how to maximize investment returns and where to live in the European Union. After the chatbot suggested buying a vineyard in Portugal, he gave Portuguese lawyers a power of attorney on June 15, 2026, to register him with the Portuguese Tax and Customs Authority, obtain a tax identification number and collect the password to his finance portal. He arranged travel to Portugal for September 2026.

The agreed statement of facts goes beyond the dollar figure. Yaroch’s conduct compromised multiple FBI investigations into criminal groups and nation-state adversaries, the office said, and it compromised FBI sources and methods used in foreign intelligence collection and criminal cases. It also “deprived FBI employees of their ability to carry out their functions to protect public safety and national security.”

First Assistant U.S. Attorney Theophani K. Stamos and Daniel S. Wierzbicki, special agent in charge of the FBI Washington Field Office’s Counterintelligence Division, announced the plea. Assistant U.S. Attorneys Kathleen Robeson and Russell Carlberg are prosecuting, with help from the National Security Cyber Section of the Justice Department’s National Security Division and the DOJ inspector general’s Mid-Atlantic region. The case is 1:26-CR-208 in the Eastern District of Virginia.

The admission lands in a week when federal crypto-crime stories keep stacking up — from a failed bitcoin kidnapping plot that produced its own guilty plea last month to FBI tallies putting U.S. crypto fraud losses above $11 billion in 2025, a figure the White House has used to frame a wider crackdown.

What sentencing will do with the gap between Yaroch’s July self-report and the $2.47 million plea figure is still open. So is how many of the 35 FBI cases can be repaired after an insider treated the bureau’s own wallet credentials as a personal ATM. March 4, 2027, is when Judge Giles gets to answer the first question.

#5199. Why your favorite candy tastes different now

Published Oct 30, 2026 9:03 AM EDT

It’s candy—I mean, Halloween—season. But, I mean, let’s be real: The two are kind of interchangeable at this point. But have you ever bitten into your favorite candy bar only to think, “This really doesn’t taste the way it used to?”

Well, it turns out, you might be onto something. Candy companies really do sneakily change their recipes. Our taste buds also change as we get older. So what’s really going on here? Are candy companies changing their recipes or do we just taste things differently now? To find out, Popular Science’s Ask Us Anything podcast took a deep dive into the surprisingly complicated science of candy.

Ask Us Anything answers your most outlandish, mind-burning questions—from the everyday things you’ve always wondered to the bizarre things you never thought to ask. So, yes, you really should wear sunscreen every day and no, you don’t need to worry about Yellowstone blowing up. If you have a question for us, send us a note. Nothing is too silly or simple.

This episode is based on the Popular Science article “Candy now tastes different. It’s not just you.”

Listen and follow Ask Us Anything on your favorite podcast platform:

Spotify | Apple Podcasts | YouTube | Or wherever you get your podcasts.

Sarah Durn: Halloween is right around the corner, and you know what that means: lots and lots of candy: Reese’s Peanut Butter Cups, Snickers, Kit Kats, Hershey’s chocolate bars. Whether you’re stocking up for trick-or-treaters or secretly buying a bag just for yourself, there’s something about Halloween that makes us crave our favorite childhood sweets.

But have you ever unwrapped a piece of candy you used to love as a kid, taken a bite, and thought, “Wait a second, this doesn’t taste like I remember it.” Maybe the chocolate tastes a little different, maybe the peanut butter filling isn’t quite as creamy, or maybe that candy bar just doesn’t hit the way it used to.

So what’s going on? Have candy companies been quietly changing the recipes of our childhood favorites, or is it possible that the candy hasn’t changed at all, and it’s actually our taste buds that are different?

Welcome to Ask Us Anything from the editors of Popular Science, where we answer your questions about our weird world, from, “Why don’t humans have tails?” to, “Why do dogs tilt their heads?” No question is too strange or simple. I’m Sarah Durn, an editor at Popular Science.

Laura Baisas: And I’m Laura Baisis, news editor at Popular Science.

SD: Here at PopSci, we’re always pondering quirky questions.

LB: And this week we’re wondering, why doesn’t candy taste as good as it used to? Sarah, is it really that companies are secretly changing the recipes for our favorite childhood treats?

SD: Candy, I know, candy companies really do change their recipes, and a lot of times they don’t even have to say so.

LB: Seriously? So, like, my beloved Milky Way could be completely different from the one that I ate when I was nine?

SD: Potentially. Companies can make tiny changes to their recipes that are almost impossible to notice, but over decades, those tiny little changes can really add up.

LB: Okay, so we’re not just imagining things.

SD: Not necessarily, but there’s another twist. Our taste buds also change as we get older, so the candy might be different, but so are we.

LB: Okay. But so who’s really to blame here, Big Candy on one side or my ever-aging taste buds on the other?

LB: Now, before we unwrap this mystery, we want to know, what questions are circling in your brain?

If there’s something you’ve always wanted to know, submit your question by clicking the “Ask Us” link at popsci.com/ask. Again, that’s popsci.com/ask, and click the “Ask Us” link.

SD: We can’t wait to hear your questions.

LB: We’ll be right back after a quick break to find out what’s really happening to our favorite candy.

SD: And stick around to the end, because we’re gonna be joined by a very special guest, Jack Bishop from America’s Test Kitchen.

LB: Yes, America’s Test Kitchen. What are we gonna ask him?

SD: Well, Jack has spent decades testing recipes and figuring out what makes food taste good, so we’re gonna put his taste buds to the test and find out what he thinks about his favorite childhood candies.

LB: Okay, now I’m officially hungry and excited.

And we’re back. Okay, Laura, before we get into whether our favorite candies have actually changed, I have a very important question for you: What was your favorite Halloween candy growing up?

LB: Oh, okay, before I reveal my top choice, I will say I love Dots.

LB: …so much so that my, my little nextdoor neighbors will save any Dots that they get while trick-or-treating for me just because I mentioned liking them one day.

SD: Aw, that’s, that’s really sweet, and I would never have shared my Halloween candy with you.

LB: But honestly, if I have to pick an all-time favorite, I’m gonna go with what I hinted at earlier, the Milky Way.

LB: They’re just, like, that perfect combination of flavors, so they never really get boring. It’s not just one note.

SD: Okay. Yeah. So I was obsessed with all Halloween candy. Like, lost-several-teeth-in-Laffy-Taffy-level obsessed. I would go trick-or-treating, come home with this absolutely enormous haul of candy, and then keep it all in a pillowcase under my bed.

SD: Uh-huh. And I would ration it out over the course of a year.

Sometimes I would still have Halloween candy left when the next Halloween rolled around.

SD: I know. I was basically running, like, a little candy warehouse under my bed. But devastatingly, once mice got into it, and that was… It was not, not the smartest hiding spot. But my brothers and sisters didn’t know where I had hid it, so I guess it evens out.

Yeah. But my favorite candy was probably Nerds. And now as an adult, I recently discovered Nerds Gummy Clusters.

LB: We’re actually gonna be handing those out this year.

SD: Oh my God, you guys are gonna be so popular.

SD: Oh my gosh. Okay. But all of this got me thinking about all the other candies I loved as a kid because there are definitely some that just don’t taste the way I remember.

And as it turns out, there’s a pretty good reason for that.

LB: Okay. Perfect. Let’s, let’s get into it. Are candy companies really messing with our childhood favorites?

SD: They are, 1000 percent. And one of the biggest recent controversies actually involves one of the most iconic Halloween candies, Reese’s Peanut Butter Cups.

SD: I know. Me too. So earlier this year, Brad Reese, the grandson of H.B. Reese, the inventor of Reese’s Peanut Butter Cups, publicly criticized Hershey, the company that makes the candy.

SD: Yeah, it’s kinda wild that there are family candy empires, but, you know, of course there are.

Anyhow, Brad was upset about changes to some of the company’s candy products. Specifically, he pointed to products that use things like compound coatings instead of milk chocolate or peanut butter-style cream instead of actual peanut butter.

LB: So in candy making, what exactly is a compound coating?

SD: It’s a chocolate-like coating that can use vegetable fats in place of some of the cocoa butter you’d find in traditional chocolate, and those substitutions can make a difference in how a candy tastes and feels in your mouth.

LB: The phrase chocolate-like kind of raises a red flag there.

LB: Okay, but here’s the really important question. Did they actually change Reese’s Peanut Butter Cups?

SD: According to Hershey, no. The company says the original Reese’s Peanut Butter Cup recipe hasn’t changed, but it acknowledges that some other Reese’s products, different shapes and sizes and newer varieties, can have different formulations.

LB: Oh, so the classic peanut butter cup shape might be the same, but the other Reese’s candies, like the popular pumpkins or Christmas tree shapes, aren’t necessarily made the same way.

SD: Yes, at least that’s the distinction the company is making. But this isn’t just about Reese’s. Candy companies have been adjusting their recipes for years, and to understand why, Popular Science spoke with Dr. Richard Hartel, a food scientist at the University of Wisconsin-Madison, and he explained that one of the biggest reasons companies change their recipes is pretty straightforward: money.

SD: Yep. Candy manufacturers are a business, and when the price of ingredients goes up, they have a pretty strong incentive to find cheaper alternatives.

But those substitutions aren’t always obvious. Take caramel, for example.

SD: Yes, same. So traditionally, caramel might be made using whole milk and milk fat. But a manufacturer could decide to replace those ingredients with skim milk and a different kind of fat, like vegetable oil or coconut oil.

They might also replace some milk proteins with whey proteins.

SD: Potentially, but it can also change the flavor and texture of the final candy. Because even if two ingredients serve a similar purpose in a recipe, that doesn’t mean they’re gonna taste exactly the same.

LB: Right, and I imagine butter and vegetable oil don’t produce exactly the same caramel just because of chemistry.

You know, it’s like vegan or regular cooking. It’s similar, but different.

SD: Yeah. No, exactly. And the same thing can happen with chocolate. A company might reduce the amount of cocoa in a product while still keeping enough to meet the legal requirement for calling it “chocolate.”

LB: So there are legal requirements for calling it chocolate, kind of like with beer?

SD: There are, yeah. The FDA has what’s called standards of identity for certain foods. Basically, these are rules that determine what ingredients a product needs to contain or how it needs to be made to legally use a particular name. So you can’t necessarily just throw some brown colored sugar and oil together and call it milk chocolate.

LB: So it’s kind of similar to that question of if almond milk can really be called “milk.” Yes. Yeah, I guess it is kind of assuring that there are some sort of standards here.

SD: Oh, yeah. No, those standards still leave manufacturers, though, room to adjust the recipes. And if a company changes its ingredients while staying within those legal requirements, it generally doesn’t have to make some big public announcement or even change their ingredient list.

LB: Right, so there’s not gonna be a big press release saying, “Attention candy lovers, we’ve slightly reduced the cocoa content of your favorite chocolate bar.”

SD: Exactly, and that’s part of what makes this whole thing so sneaky. Because according to Hartel, some of these recipe changes are so subtle that even trained testers might not notice them.

LB: Really? So like even those lucky people whose job it is to literally taste things?

SD: Yep. Imagine you have a candy bar, you know, you’ve eaten your whole life. One year the company makes a tiny adjustment to the recipe. You probably don’t notice. The next year they make another tiny adjustment. Again, you probably don’t notice.

But if you keep doing that over 10 years or 20 years…

LB: Okay, so that explains why a candy bar might taste different now. But here’s what I’m wondering. If people notice these changes and don’t like them, why don’t companies just go back to the original recipes?

SD: Yeah, well that brings us to one of the most famous disasters in the history of food marketing: New Coke.

SD: So back in 1985, Coca-Cola decided to change its signature soda formula, and the company had actually done taste testing beforehand. In blind taste tests, people often preferred sweeter Pepsi over Coke. So Coca-Cola introduced a new sweeter version of its drink.

LB: Which as we now know, people famously hated.

SD: Hated it. And it’s all because of something called the Pepsi paradox. Basically, there’s a difference between tasting a tiny sip of something and actually consuming an entire serving. A sweeter drink might win in a quick sip test, but that doesn’t necessarily mean people want to drink a whole can.

LB: Right. That makes sense. Like if one bite of something is super sweet, it might be delicious—

LB: —but after 10 bites you might be completely over it.

SD: Yeah, and that’s a big challenge for food companies because just because a new recipe performs well in a taste test doesn’t mean consumers will actually prefer it in their everyday lives.

And with New Coke there was another huge factor.

LB: Okay. If it’s not money, I’m gonna guess it’s nostalgia.

SD: Nostalgia. People were attached to the original Coca-Cola. It wasn’t just about how the soda tasted. It was about the memories and feelings associated with it.

LB: All right, and that brings us right back to Halloween candy.

SD: Yeah. When you eat a Reese’s Peanut Butter Cup or a Hershey’s bar, you’re not necessarily just tasting chocolate and sugar and peanut butter.

LB: You might also be remembering the fun of trick-or-treating as a kid, dumping your candy all over the living room floor and trading with your siblings.

SD: Or in my case, hiding an entire year’s supply of candy under my bed.

LB: Hey, I now think it’s very important to have a secret candy bunker.

SD: Look, I concur. And that brings us to another important part of this mystery because even if a candy company hasn’t changed its recipe at all, there’s still something else that could be changing.

SD: Us. Our sense of taste isn’t necessarily the same throughout our lives.

As we get older, our ability to perceive flavors can change, and one reason is that our taste buds don’t regenerate quite as quickly as they used to.

LB: So even if I’m eating a Milky Way, I might experience it differently now.

SD: Right, even if it’s the same exact Milky Way. And it’s not just aging. Our environment and experiences also affect how we perceive taste.

So the way you experience a Milky Way today might be different from the way you experienced it as a kid, even if the recipe is identical.

LB: Okay, so with this mystery, we’ve now got two possible culprits: the candy companies are changing the recipes, and our taste buds are changing too.

SD: And both things can be true at the same time, which makes it difficult to know whether your beloved Milky Way or addictive Nerds actually taste different or whether you just remember it tasting different.

LB: So basically, no matter how much we want to, we’re never getting that exact childhood Halloween candy experience back, no matter how much we try.

SD: I know, but there’s a little good news. Candy companies do sometimes listen when people complain about changes. In fact, Hershey has announced plans to bring back older recipes for some of its products, including certain miniature Easter candies.

LB: Okay, that helps. So there’s some hope.

SD: There is, but Hershey will still experiment with new formulas for other products, so I wouldn’t expect every candy to suddenly taste exactly the way it did in 1998.

SD: But here’s the big takeaway. If you’ve bitten into your favorite childhood candy and thought, “This isn’t as good as it used to be,” you might actually be onto something.

Manufacturers really do change the recipes. Sometimes those changes are tiny, and sometimes they accumulate over decades, but our own taste perception changes too. So the answer to whether candy tastes different now is—

SD: It really is. But before we wrap up this very sweet episode, there’s one thing I want to get to the bottom of because we spent this whole episode talking about how our favorite childhood candies might be changing, but what would someone who tastes food for a living think?

LB: I feel like we need to bring in the expert here.

SD: Me too, and luckily, we got a pretty great one. After the break, we’re gonna be joined by Jack Bishop from America’s Test Kitchen.

LB: Excellent. Love America’s Test Kitchen.

And we’re back with Jack Bishop. Jack is the senior content advisor at America’s Test Kitchen, where he’s been part of the team since the very beginning, starting with the launch of Cook’s Illustrated in 1993. He’s also the tasting lab expert on America’s Test Kitchen and Cook’s Country, which means he’s spent decades putting food to the test.

Jack, thank you so much for joining us on Popular Science’s Ask Us Anything podcast.

Jack Bishop: It’s great to be here, Sarah. Thanks for asking me.

SD: Oh, we’re so excited to have you. So Jack, throughout today’s episode, we’ve been investigating whether our favorite childhood candies really taste different than they used to, and I want to start with a personal question.

Was there a candy you absolutely loved growing up that just doesn’t quite taste as good to you today?

JB: When you would bite into a Snickers bar, it was almost like a cheese pull. You know, you’d get this great pull, and I feel like that doesn’t happen anymore.

SD: And given your knowledge of food generally, why do you think Snickers taste different?

JB: —you know, you can tell by the order of the ingredients. If that changes, that could be a real sign. You know, if something has gone up in the ingredient list, that likely means there’s more of it, because ingredients are listed in the order of sort of weight. You know, they’re just using lower quality ingredients in order to keep costs down.

I mean, that’s the overall trend in what I would call supermarket candy.

And so, you know, using less of the expensive ingredient, switching out, you know, a moderately priced ingredient for a really cheap ingredient. Those are all strategies that could be used to control cost, but they’re not necessarily gonna make something tastier.

The food is really triggering powerful emotions.

JB: But, you know, we misremember stuff, and it’s really hard to remember, well, how sweet was this?

JB: Right? How chocolatey was this? Was this really peanut buttery, or have my tastes just changed? And I know for me, you know, I now like 85 percent cacao chocolate.

Well, I didn’t like that as a kid. And so that will color perceptions of things I did eat when I was a kid, and is it not as good as it used to be, or do I not like that anymore? Unless you can get a time machine and somebody can send you a candy bar from, you know, 1972 or whatever year you’d like to go back to, you’re relying on your memory, and your memory’s a little fickle when it comes to food.

SD: Yeah, and we’ve actually been talking a bit in this episode about Reese’s Peanut Butter Cups because they’ve been at the center of this whole conversation about changing candy recipes, and I know America’s Test Kitchen actually has its own recipe for homemade peanut butter cups. So I’m curious, what makes a really great peanut butter cup? Is there something the homemade version can get right that a store-bought candy might not?

JB: Fewer ingredients and better ingredients. So, you know, if you read the label on a Reese’s Peanut Butter Cup, it’s a pretty long ingredient list. Our homemade version starts with milk chocolate and creamy peanut butter that you enrich with a little bit of butter, a little bit of salt, and a little bit of confectioner sugar ‘cause the confectioner sugar will incorporate into the sugar without giving you any sort of grittiness, and that’s it.

It’s really just creamy peanut butter that’s a little sweeter and a little saltier and a little richer than it is straight out of the jar layered with melted chocolate, and you need, don’t need to anything more than put the chocolate in the microwave, 50 percent power to gently melt it, and then you can actually build them.

The easiest way to do this is in a mini muffin tin.

JB: And you can get little liners, and you sort of pipe a layer of chocolate, pipe a layer of peanut butter, and then finish with a layer of chocolate. So the chocolate’s not completely enrobing the peanut butter, but it’s the neatest, easiest way to do it at home, and it eats like…

Well, I won’t say it eats like a Reese’s Peanut Butter Cup ‘cause it’s so much better.

#5198. Google Tells Schools They Can Reject AI—But They'll Lose Search, Too

Google Tells Schools They Can Reject AI—But They'll Lose Search, Too

Google is giving schools more freedom to reject artificial intelligence—provided they're also willing to give up Google Search, which is arguably what everybody in the world uses Google for.

The company announced Tuesday that its AI products will be disabled by default for K-12 student accounts unless administrators explicitly enable them. But schools that want to ban AI entirely will also have to disable Google's search engine, a tool millions of students rely on for everyday research.

Chris Phillips, Google's vice president and general manager of education, tried to justify the reasoning during a meeting with educators.

“Search and AI are deeply integrated,” he said according to Education Week. Schools uncomfortable with AI can choose not to enable Search at all.

Google says administrators will be able to activate or disable Gemini, Gemini Notebook, Gemini in Classroom, and Search individually. That means schools can block the standalone chatbots while keeping Search available, but they cannot remove AI from Search itself.

For teachers who want students to research sources without receiving AI-generated answers, the company is effectively offering two options: accept its AI-powered search or find another search engine.

The announcement is already drawing accusations that Google is using its dominance in education to push a technology many schools are still trying to understand, regulate or restrict.

Google says the changes are intended to address concerns that AI chatbots can undermine learning by giving students ready-made answers instead of encouraging them to solve problems themselves.

Beginning in early 2027, schools that enable Search will be offered versions with conventional AI features, such as AI Overviews and AI Mode, or the education-focused Guided Learning experience, according to The New York Times.

Neither option provides the traditional search experience without generative AI.

New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani accused the company of “trying to use its monopoly power to force AI into our public schools,” according to CBS News.

Former Federal Trade Commission chair Lina Khan was equally critical, accusing Google of using its search monopoly to coerce schools into adopting AI.

The criticism comes as some of America's largest school districts are actively restricting generative AI. New York City, for example, imposed a one-year moratorium on student-facing generative AI through eighth grade, covering nearly 600,000 students, while Los Angeles has also restricted the technology.

Los Angeles officials had specifically hoped to preserve Google Search while removing AI-generated summaries. Under the newly announced controls, that won't be an option.

Google's position also arrives amid continuing antitrust scrutiny. A federal court ruled in 2024 that Google unlawfully monopolized the U.S. search market, and the company has faced separate legal challenges over its decision to integrate AI summaries into search results.

School administrators are expected to receive the new controls in early 2027. They will be able to decide which AI products students can access, and even apply different settings by grade level. A Google Search option without AI, however, is not among the choices.

That said, for schools that want to, ahem, unofficially ban AI without losing Google search, our guide may provide a quick solution. It takes 5 minutes and lasts forever.

#5197. Base deploys Denim upgrade on mainnet, replacing Flashblocks with 200ms block times

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Denim retires Flashblocks, adds millisecond-resolution timestamps, and updates B20 transfer and compliance controls.

Base's fourth mainnet upgrade, Denim, is live, with the Coinbase-incubated Layer 2 now producing a full canonical block every 200 milliseconds, or five per second, the project said in a statement shared with The Block on Thursday.

The upgrade also introduces millisecond-resolution timestamps for smart contracts, alongside new transfer protections and compliance controls for tokens using Base's B20 standard.

Base said the upgrade retires Flashblocks and changes how the pending block tag works, making block production "simpler, more consistent, and more reliable." Each block now follows its parent exactly 200 milliseconds later, with the protocol enforcing the interval rather than relying on the sequencer's clock.

Applications that previously relied on Flashblocks must migrate to standard canonical block subscriptions, according to the statement.

"Base’s north star is to bring the world onchain, starting with trading, financing, and payments," the statement read. "Those use cases need a chain that updates state often and lets builders reason about time precisely."

According to the statement, the upgrade also introduces BaseTime, which gives smart contracts access to Unix timestamps in milliseconds. Ethereum-compatible block.timestamp remains unchanged at whole-second resolution, while new RPC fields provide millisecond timestamps for blocks, transactions, and logs.

Denim also introduces reusable compliance policies through a NOT operator and extends transfer-executor restrictions to direct transfers. It also blocks transfers, mints and seizures that send tokens to the token contract's own address, Base said.

The upgrade follows Azul, Beryl and Cobalt, Base's first three independent upgrades since its 2023 launch. Azul introduced a new client stack and multiproof system, while Beryl brought the B20 token standard. Cobalt added conditional transactions and expanded B20 compliance features.

#5194. Thailand SEC Files Criminal Complaint Against Bitget Ahead of Dec. 5 Access Block

Thailand's SEC filed a criminal complaint against Bitget operator BG Limited for unlicensed Thai trading from 2022 to 2025.

Thailand’s Securities and Exchange Commission filed a criminal complaint Thursday against BG Limited, the operator of cryptocurrency exchange Bitget, alleging it ran an unlicensed digital-asset exchange for Thai users from 2022 to 2025, and notified the government to block the platform from Dec. 5.

The complaint, lodged with the Economic Crime Suppression Division, also names Mr. Kritsana Kritsananuwat as an alleged supporter who promoted Bitget through Facebook and YouTube channels, the SEC said in statement No. 241/2026.

From 2022 through 2025, the regulator found, Bitget offered a trading system in the ordinary course of business, charged fees, ran a Thai-language website that could show values in baht, and solicited Thai users on social channels including “Bitget Thailand” on X, “BGB Thai Holder” on Telegram and “bitgetthailand” on TikTok.

That conduct amounts to operating a digital-asset exchange under Section 3 of the Emergency Decree on Digital Asset Businesses B.E. 2561 (2018) without a license, a violation of Section 26 punishable under Section 66, the SEC said. The case now moves to an inquiry official, then prosecutors and the courts.

Separately, the SEC submitted Bitget’s website, other domains and its app to the Ministry of Digital Economy and Society under a 2025 royal decree that authorizes access blocks on unlicensed trading platforms. From Dec. 5, the public “will be unable to access the platform,” the statement said, and investors still holding assets there were told to consider acting before the cutoff.

The enforcement push lands weeks after Bitget announced a planned acquisition of GMO-Z.com Cryptonomics (Thailand), a Bangkok digital-asset exchange and brokerage that already holds Thai licenses, through its interest in BTGT Management (Thailand) Ltd.

Under that deal, GMO Internet and its Thai holding company would transfer their entire stake in GMO-Z — about 49% and 50.94%, respectively — to BTGT or a designee at completion, a Sept. 24 GMO Internet filing showed. Bitget said completion remains subject to regulatory approval and, after a change-of-control nod, it intends to introduce its trading platform to Thai users under local rules.

“Thailand is an important part of Southeast Asia’s digital asset landscape, and this acquisition gives us a great opportunity to establish local operations,” Bitget CEO Gracy Chen said in the Oct. 20 release.

The timetable has already slipped. GMO Internet Group pushed the effective date of the share transfer from Oct. 31 to Nov. 30, citing careful work on Thai regulatory applications and closing procedures. That revised date sits five days before the planned Bitget access block.

CoinDesk reported that Bitget’s own terms of use, updated in October, already list Thailand among jurisdictions where its services are prohibited, and that the SEC has not said whether the criminal complaint will weigh on the GMO-Z change of control.

Thailand has used the same access-blocking tool before. In mid-2025 the SEC referred Bybit, OKX and three other unlicensed platforms for a June 28 cutoff, telling users to move assets first. The Bitget action follows that template — and adds a criminal complaint against the exchange operator and a named promoter.

Licensed venues still sit at the center of Bangkok’s policy mix. Thailand has approved a multi-year capital-gains tax break only for trades on SEC-regulated platforms, and has been expanding regulated crypto products in derivatives and capital markets. Unlicensed platforms get the opposite treatment: investor alerts, access blocks and, now for Bitget, a police referral.

What is not yet clear is whether a criminal case against BG Limited for the 2022–2025 period bars, slows or leaves untouched BTGT’s bid to take over a licensed local operator. The SEC statement does not mention GMO-Z. Bitget has not publicly answered the complaint in a statement posted with the acquisition release.

For Thai users still on Bitget, the practical clock is Dec. 5. For Bitget, the test is whether Nov. 30 closes a regulated door into Thailand — or whether the complaint filed Thursday makes that licensed path the next question the ECD and the SEC have to answer.

#5193. ChatGPT Is Getting a GIF Maker Powered by OpenAI's Unannounced Images 2.6 Model

ChatGPT Is Getting a GIF Maker Powered by OpenAI's Unannounced Images 2.6 Model

The internet may soon have an unlimited supply of AI-generated dancing cats, courtesy of ChatGPT.

OpenAI is rolling out a feature that lets users generate animated GIFs directly inside ChatGPT, according to The Verge. The feature reportedly comes powered by its new image-generation model called Images 2.6, which OpenAI has yet to formally introduce through its usual product announcements.

Users can describe an animation in plain English or upload an existing photo and ask ChatGPT to bring it to life. Once the GIF is generated, they can adjust its playback speed, inspect individual frames, make edits, and share the finished animation.

OpenAI demonstrated the capability with a simple request: “can you make a gif of a cat dancing?” The resulting interface reportedly includes controls to change animation speed and examine the GIF frame by frame.

We tried generating GIFs using ChatGPT, but the feature was not available on our accounts at the time of testing. In our interaction, the model generates a series of frames, but not the animation.

The rollout appears to be gradual, so users may have to keep asking for their dancing cats a little longer.

The GIF maker is reportedly part of a broader Images 2.6 update that promises faster image generation. The Verge reports that OpenAI is bringing the model to ChatGPT and Codex users across web, desktop, and mobile platforms.

Developers are also getting two new API models, GPT-Image-2.6 Flare and GPT-Image-2.6 Sunburst, designed for faster generation and higher-quality results, respectively. OpenAI has not yet published a standalone announcement detailing the new model's capabilities, and we could not independently test its performance.

The update follows the September release of ChatGPT Images 2.5, which introduced sharper details, more precise editing, and generation speeds up to 50% faster than its predecessor.

In our previous testing, Images 2.5 went head-to-head with Google's Nano Banana 2 across six categories, with each model winning three. OpenAI's model delivered particularly impressive illustration quality and improved on the oversharpening problems that affected earlier versions, although it still made mistakes when rendering text.

The move into GIF generation also comes months after OpenAI shut down Sora, its standalone AI video-generation app, in March. Animated GIFs are considerably simpler than the videos Sora generated, but the new feature brings a form of motion generation directly into ChatGPT without requiring a separate application.

OpenAI has not provided a specific date for when every ChatGPT account will receive GIF generation.

#5192. Bitcoin erases post-election gains as Wall Street selloff triggers over $1 billion in crypto liquidations

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The selloff triggered a wave of forced liquidations across crypto derivatives markets, as total liquidations over the past 24 hours reached $1.09 billion, affecting 211,000 traders.

Bitcoin, Ethereum, and other cryptocurrencies erased their post-election gains on Thursday as a sharp selloff in U.S. technology and AI stocks spilled into cryptocurrency markets, triggering more than $1 billion in leveraged position liquidations over the past 24 hours.

Bitcoin (BTC) fell 6% to $75,700, after touching an intraday low of $75,568, while ether (ETH) dropped more than 7% to $2,247. The declines reversed Wednesday's rally, when bitcoin climbed back above $80,000 following the U.S. midterm elections and signs that the Binance hack had not caused broader market disruption.

Midterm election results appeared to generate short-term optimism, but analysts say they haven't resolved macroeconomic uncertainties.

As of 2 p.m. ET, the Dow Jones Industrial Average and S&P 500 were down about 3% for the day, while the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite had dropped 4.7%. The Cboe Volatility Index, or VIX, surged approximately 60%, reflecting heightened investor anxiety.

AI and bitcoin-related mining stocks were among the hardest hit, with Bitdeer and Keel Infrastructure each falling about 20%. American Bitcoin dropped 17% despite announcing a quarterly profit of $126 million. Crypto bellwethers Strategy and Coinbase were each down about 10%.

Of note, the Official Trump (TRUMP) memecoin hit an all-time low after falling more than 20% over the past two days.

The selloff also triggered a wave of forced liquidations across crypto derivatives markets. About $879.92 million in positions were liquidated over a four-hour period, including $818.90 million in longs and $61.02 million in shorts, according to CoinGlass. Total liquidations over the past 24 hours reached nearly $1.1 billion, affecting nearly 211,000 traders.

It should be noted that liquidation figures are typically based on publicly available data, which may significantly understate the extent of market liquidations.

The reversal suggests Wednesday's rebound may have been driven more by temporary optimism than a meaningful improvement in the market's underlying conditions, according to Wintermute.

The trading firm said the rally reflected a combination of relief following the Binance hack, enthusiasm surrounding the election results, optimism about a potential slowdown in AI spending and falling oil prices. However, those factors proved short-lived as broader macroeconomic pressures persisted.

"It's probably a combination of all of them in some way. What matters is that all of them seemed to be so short-lived that it actually didn't really matter," said Jasper De Maere, an OTC trader at Wintermute. "Trump is still the president for two years, the Middle East is still escalating, inflation is elevated into a strong U.S. economy."

Beyond the immediate market pressures, the election results may also complicate the outlook for crypto regulation, potentially tempering some of the optimism that helped drive Wednesday's rebound.

Paul Howard, senior director at Wincent, said the Democrats' strengthened position in Congress could make bipartisan cooperation necessary for meaningful progress on crypto legislation over the next two years. With the Clarity Act's prospects uncertain, Howard expects the existing regulatory framework to largely remain intact until the next election cycle.

Ryan Kirkley, CEO of Global Settlement Network, said the election could delay crypto legislation but wouldn't fundamentally change longer-term institutional demand for digital assets.

"Investors are still focused on liquidity, interest rates and the broader economy, and those factors will continue to influence the market regardless of which party controls Congress," Kirkley said.

If the weakness continues, Wintermute identified $73,000 and $68,000 as the next levels to watch for bitcoin. The firm said it would need to see bitcoin convincingly reclaim and hold $80,000 before turning more positive.

#5189. Why Bitcoin's Next Soft Fork Could Feel Like a Practice Run

Why Bitcoin's Next Soft Fork Could Feel Like a Practice Run

Four flaws in Bitcoin’s consensus rules that have lingered for years.

Issues like quantum resistance may command attention, but a practical cleanup is what many experts think Bitcoin’s next soft fork is likely to address, spanning fixes for vulnerabilities in the network’s difficulty adjustment mechanism to denial-of-service vectors among miners.

Proposed in 2019 and known as the Great Consensus Cleanup (BIP-54), the upgrade seeks to address the “cruft” in Bitcoin’s consensus design through changes that have historically been sidelined, as described by long-time Bitcoin developer and BIP-54 co-author Matt Corallo.

“There’s a laundry list of things we know of that we really need to clean up,” he said during a recent appearance on MARA Foundation TV. “All of these issues are real concerns that could impact Bitcoin negatively, but they’re not like, ‘Oh, the sky is falling.’”

Although a formal timeline for the BIP’s undertaking hasn’t been established, Corallo thinks there’s an 80% chance that the Great Consensus Cleanup is up next for the network. During his talk with Head of MARA Foundation Isabel Foxen Duke, he also called it a practical exercise for Bitcoin developers who haven’t yet been through a soft fork.

At its core, Corallo said BIP-54 “fixes some stuff that everyone agrees are problems,” while targeting four specific vulnerabilities:

Still, not everyone views a maintenance-focused soft fork as a major win. On X, Paul Sztorc, co-founder and CEO of LayerTwo Labs, called BIP-54’s potential activation “kind of depressing, since it is a janitorial maintenance fork that doesn't do anything.”

Surveying developers at TABconf in Atlanta, Sztorc noted that Bitcoin’s “best and brightest” predominantly expect the Great Consensus Cleanup to be Bitcoin’s next soft fork anyway, with the earliest estimate in September 2027, and most pointing to 2028 or later.

Following the activation of Taproot in 2021, Bitcoin’s community has faced years of gridlock surrounding protocol changes. This friction is frequently framed around “ossification,” with broader protocol changes like OP_CAT (BIP-347) and OP_CHECKTEMPLATEVERIFY (BIP-119) struggling to reach ecosystem-wide consensus.

According to Corallo, soft forks have always involved overcoming technical hurdles. But more recently, he’s observed political and social friction that’s become just as relevant.

The Bitcoin community spent months debating how Taproot should be activated, with arguments pitting miner-signaling thresholds against mandatory user flag days. Corallo argues that BIP-54 can establish a deployment playbook for future soft forks.

“In every recent soft fork, there’s also been a bunch of drama about activation mechanisms,” he said. “So hopefully we could do a soft fork to go through that drama, and then copy and paste and reuse it and head off some of that drama [for future soft forks].”

In that sense, BIP-54 is just as much about proving Bitcoin can still agree on upgrades at the social layer as it is about actual code cleanup.

André Beganski is a Senior Content Manager at MARA Foundation, producing content on topics including the intersection of quantum computing and cryptography. He has previously worked for Decrypt.

#5186. U.S. Treasury Cuts Off MEXC From U.S. Financial System Over Alleged $16 Billion in Illicit Flows

The U.S. Treasury’s Financial Crimes Enforcement Network issued a final order Friday that will cut offshore exchange MEXC Global off from the American banking system over alleged links to sanctions evasion by Iran, North Korea and Russia, the department said.

FinCEN’s order, published as a PDF pending Federal Register placement, finds MEXC a “primary money laundering concern in connection with Russian illicit finance” under section 9714(a) of the Combating Russian Money Laundering Act. Thirty days after that Register notice, covered U.S. financial institutions will be barred from any funds transfer — including convertible virtual currency — to or from MEXC.

Reuters reported the same cut-off Friday afternoon. Treasury framed the move as part of Operation Economic Outcast. It is not an OFAC sanctions designation: the order says it does not rewrite Bank Secrecy Act duties or OFAC rules, and it is a FinCEN funds-transmittal ban rather than a Section 311 proposed rulemaking.

Treasury alleges MEXC facilitated at least $16 billion in illicit flows from May 2021 through May 2026, including nearly $1.5 billion tied to Russian sanctions evasion and about $22 million linked to Iran’s Central Bank and the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps’ Qods Force. The platform was still processing trades with sanctioned Garantex and successor Grinex as recently as June 2026, more than four years after OFAC designated Garantex, the release says.

The order, signed by FinCEN Deputy Director Jimmy L. Kirby, covers MEXC Global and named affiliates operating outside the United States, including Seychelles-incorporated MEXC Global Ltd / MX Global Ltd, Estonia’s MEXC Estonia OÜ, Switzerland’s GIDA Technology AG and the U.K.’s MXC Global Ltd, plus successors. Covered institutions face civil or criminal penalties for willful violations.

Until the Federal Register publishes the notice, the 30-day clock has not started. As of Friday evening eastern time the Register’s public index still showed no MEXC document, matching the order’s own cover note that publication is pending.

Founded in 2018 and formerly branded MXC, MEXC ranks among the world’s 20 largest centralized crypto venues by volume. Treasury puts daily turnover above $1 billion and reserves near $5 billion as of mid-2026, with access claimed across more than 170 countries. The exchange already lists the United States as a prohibited jurisdiction in its user terms, so Friday’s action mainly hits any remaining dollar rails, banking partners and counterparties that still touch U.S. institutions.

Regulators elsewhere have been circling for years. The U.K. Financial Conduct Authority put MEXC Global Ltd on its warning list in March 2024 as unauthorized. Hong Kong’s Securities and Futures Commission warned the same month that the platform was marketing to local investors without a licence. Seychelles’ Financial Services Authority said in May 2026 it took action against MX Global Ltd for unlicensed virtual-asset operations — footnoted in FinCEN’s order.

Those compliance gaps matter because the illicit-flow thesis is specific, not guilt by association. Beyond Russia, FinCEN cites about $1 billion in direct traffic with more than 40 Iranian crypto exchangers, nearly $26 million with the OFAC-sanctioned A7 sanctions-evasion network, and almost $200 million tied to the A7A5 stablecoin. DPRK facilitators and cybercrime flows are also alleged. MEXC has not issued a public response to the Friday order; its main X account kept posting marketing content through the afternoon.

Practically, once the order bites, U.S. banks, money transmitters and other covered firms cannot move dollars or crypto to MEXC wallets or accounts. Offshore users may keep trading peer-to-peer, but dollar on-ramps and any bank that still clears for MEXC become legal risk. The template tracks earlier 9714 cut-offs of venues such as Bitzlato, and it lands beside a busy sanctions week that has already seen North Korea crypto designations, an Iranian exchange action, and Russia’s push to put bitcoin trading on the Moscow Exchange.

Whether MEXC contests the finding, seeks an exemption, or quietly reroutes liquidity is the open question. For now the paper trail is clear: a final FinCEN order under 9714(a), a 30-day fuse after Federal Register publication, and Treasury’s claim of at least $16 billion in illicit volume sitting behind the cut-off.

#5185. Arkansas Sues Kalshi, Joining a Growing List of States Targeting Prediction Markets

Arkansas Sues Kalshi, Joining a Growing List of States Targeting Prediction Markets

Arkansas has sued Kalshi, accusing the prediction market of offering an illegal, unregulated gambling platform to the state's residents, Attorney General Tim Griffin said on Wednesday.

The lawsuit, filed against KalshiEX LLC and Kalshi Inc., alleges multiple violations of the Arkansas Deceptive Trade Practices Act. The state is seeking restitution, penalties, taxes and an injunction.

Today I filed a lawsuit against @Kalshi for violating the Arkansas Deceptive Trade Practices Act by operating an unregulated gambling platform.

Kalshi has built a global business that it calls a prediction market, allowing consumers to wager money on the outcome of just about… pic.twitter.com/ePycNKXzy6

— Attorney General Tim Griffin (@AGTimGriffin) November 4, 2026

"It doesn't take a genius to understand that wagering money on whether the Razorbacks win or lose is gambling," Griffin said. "And gambling on an unregulated platform is squarely prohibited by Arkansas law."

Instead of obtaining licenses and paying the taxes required of gambling operators, Kalshi opened its platform to Arkansas residents as young as 18 and encouraged them to bet on sporting and entertainment events, Griffin alleged. "Arkansas law is concerned with substance, not semantics," he said.

Griffin pointed to Kalshi's own responsible-gambling tools, including self-exclusion, deposit limits and trading breaks, along with its partnership with the National Council on Problem Gambling, as evidence that the company knows its platform carries gambling-style risks. "Although Kalshi insists that its products are not gambling, its actions speak louder than its labels," he said.

Kalshi, which is regulated by the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, has argued in other cases that its event contracts fall under exclusive federal jurisdiction and are not subject to state gambling laws.

Arkansas is the latest of more than a dozen states to take on prediction markets. Among the most significant cases:

Other states have also moved against prediction markets. Arizona filed criminal charges against Kalshi in March, a Washington court temporarily blocked its sports contracts in July, and Massachusetts' case is now before the state's highest court after a lower court barred Kalshi's sports contracts in January.

The CFTC has sued at least 10 states to defend what it says is its exclusive authority over prediction markets, and President Donald Trump has backed the agency, calling several state officials who challenged the platforms "scum."

Federal appeals courts are split. The Third Circuit ruled in April that New Jersey cannot regulate Kalshi's sports contracts, while the Sixth and Ninth Circuits sided with Ohio, Tennessee and Nevada. New Jersey has asked the Supreme Court to settle the question, backed by 39 other states and the District of Columbia, as well as the NFL. Kalshi's response to the petition is due November 9, and the Supreme Court is not expected to decide whether to take up the case until December at the earliest, ESPN reported.
